+`fixest` is an R package offering fast and flexible econometric estimations. It provides a unified
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:11: trailing whitespace.
+framework for applied research, with comprehensive support for a diverse class of models:
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:12: trailing whitespace.
+ordinary least squares (OLS), instrumental variables (IV), generalized linear models (GLM), maximum
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:13: trailing whitespace.
+likelihood (ML), and difference-in-differences (DiD).
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:14: trailing whitespace.
+
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:15: trailing whitespace.
+## Features
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:16: trailing whitespace.
+
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:17: trailing whitespace.
+A few features:
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:18: trailing whitespace.
+- super fast
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:19: trailing whitespace.
+- large array of models (OLS, IV, GLM, ML) with a consistent syntax throughout
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:20: trailing whitespace.
+- highly optimised handling of fixed-effects
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:21: trailing whitespace.
+- 6 built-in family of VCOVs for inference
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:22: trailing whitespace.
+- run multiple estimations with minimal typing, at dazzling speed
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:23: trailing whitespace.
+- integrated panel features (lead/lag/diff)
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:24: trailing whitespace.
+- top notch error handling to facilitate the user's life
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:25: trailing whitespace.
+- programmatic formula manipulation with built-in interpolation and macro variables
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:26: trailing whitespace.
+- compatible with > 20 `base`/`stats` methods augmented with numerous options (`predict`, etc)
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:27: trailing whitespace.
+- methods to extract parts of the, possibly multiple, results (`coeftable`, `se`, `pvalue`, `tstat`)
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:28: trailing whitespace.
+- easy reporting of multiple results on the console, or in publication-ready Latex tables
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:29: trailing whitespace.
+- coefficient plots, including event-study graphs

+## Benchmarks
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:151: trailing whitespace.
+
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:152: trailing whitespace.
+Benchmarks should never be taken at face value given that the timings depend on many parameters: the hardware, the software versions installed, and the specific numerical setups. However, with these caveats in mind, they can still be informative. Below is a set of benchmarks on simulated data with simple/difficult fixed-effects convergence properties, run on 12 January 2026 with up to date software at that time:
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:153: trailing whitespace.
+
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:154: trailing whitespace.
+![](https://github.com/lrberge/fixest/blob/master/vignettes/images/readme/bench_ols_simple.png?raw=true)
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:155: trailing whitespace.
+![](https://github.com/lrberge/fixest/blob/master/vignettes/images/readme/bench_ols_difficult.png?raw=true)
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:156: trailing whitespace.
+![](https://github.com/lrberge/fixest/blob/master/vignettes/images/readme/bench_poisson_simple.png?raw=true)
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:157: trailing whitespace.
+![](https://github.com/lrberge/fixest/blob/master/vignettes/images/readme/bench_poisson_difficult.png?raw=true)
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:158: trailing whitespace.
+
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:159: trailing whitespace.
+Please refer to "Section 8 Benchmarks" of the [fixest paper](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.21749) for details on the setup. The code to run the benchmarks is [on zenodo](https://zenodo.org/records/20704145). See "Section 8.2 NYC taxi data" for a benchmark highlighting `fixest`-specific features with a large real-world data set.
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:160: trailing whitespace.
+
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:161: trailing whitespace.
+Across the board, it is safe to say that `fixest` is one of the fastest tools out there to perform econometric estimations with fixed-effects. Julia's [`FixedEffectModels`](https://github.com/FixedEffects/FixedEffectModels.jl) is very close and might be faster on specific contexts. Even if python's [`pyfixest`](https://github.com/py-econometrics/pyfixest) is slower on these benchmarks, this is likely to change in the close future: Recently the `pyfixest` team, led by Alex Fisher, has been very active in the development of new demeaning algorithms and have come up with new, and very creative, solutions. In particular for 3+ fixed-effects with difficult convergence properties, you should [check it out](https://pyfixest.org/how-to/demeaner-backends.html) if interested.
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:162: trailing whitespace.
+
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:163: trailing whitespace.
+## Acknowledgements
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:164: trailing whitespace.
+
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:165: trailing whitespace.
+The development of `fixest` has been inspired and pushed forward by many existing software. In particular, the formula syntax with the pipe to separate the fixed-effects from the independent variables is not from me but from Simen Gaure's [`lfe`](https://cran.r-project.org/package=lfe). I learned a lot on R-language manipulation by perusing the source code of Achim Zeileis' [`Formula`](https://cran.r-project.org/package=Formula) package. I was spurred to improve `fixest`'s algorithms by a sane (yet intense!) competition: first by `lfe`, then by Julia's `FixedEffectModels`.
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:166: trailing whitespace.
+
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:167: trailing whitespace.
+I am truly indebted to the community who, with countless -- and often of very high quality -- bug reports, has tremendously improved the robustness of the software.
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:168: trailing whitespace.
+
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:169: trailing whitespace.
+Big thanks to the direct contributors (esp. Grant, Kyle, Sebastian) for taking the time to contribute to this project with their expertise.
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_root_2.txt:170: trailing whitespace.

+#' The stepwise function `sw` will estimate the following two models: `y ~ x1 + x2` and 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_0.txt:177: trailing whitespace.
+#' `y ~ x1 + x3`. That is, each element in `sw()` is sequentially, and separately, 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_0.txt:178: trailing whitespace.
+#' added to the formula. Would have you used `sw0` in lieu of `sw`, then the model 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_0.txt:179: trailing whitespace.
+#' `y ~ x1` would also have been estimated. The `0` in the name means that the model 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_0.txt:181: trailing whitespace.
+#' The prefix `c` means cumulative: each stepwise element is added to the next. That is, 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_0.txt:182: trailing whitespace.
+#' `fml = y ~ x1 + csw(x2, x3)` would lead to the following models `y ~ x1 + x2` and 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_0.txt:183: trailing whitespace.
+#' `y ~ x1 + x2 + x3`. The `0` has the same meaning and would also lead to the model without 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_0.txt:184: trailing whitespace.
+#' the stepwise elements to be estimated: in other words, `fml = y ~ x1 + csw0(x2, x3)` 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_0.txt:186: trailing whitespace.
+#' Finally `mvsw` will add, in a stepwise fashion all possible combinations of the variables 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_0.txt:187: trailing whitespace.
+#' in its arguments. For example `mvsw(x1, x2, x3)` is equivalent to 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_0.txt:188: trailing whitespace.

docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_0.txt:191: trailing whitespace.

docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_0.txt:208: trailing whitespace.

docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_0.txt:211: trailing whitespace.

+The regression coefficient takes the form 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_7.txt:43: trailing whitespace.

docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_7.txt:54: trailing whitespace.
+where $\Sigma = \mathbb{E} \left[ \varepsilon \varepsilon' \right]$ is the covariance matrix of the error term. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_7.txt:56: trailing whitespace.
+Continuing with the name, $\left( X' X \right)^{-1}$ show up on each side and are called the "bread". 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_7.txt:57: trailing whitespace.
+The bread is immediately estimated from the data. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_7.txt:70: trailing whitespace.
+The elements $\sigma_{i,j}$ denote the covariance between unit $i$ and unit $j$'s error term: $\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_i \varepsilon_j]$. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_7.txt:72: trailing whitespace.

+vignette: > 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:19: trailing whitespace.
+  %\VignetteIndexEntry{On collinearity} 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:34: trailing whitespace.
+Without further ado, let's try to remove chance from the econometrics process and try to understand what collinearity is and how to deal with it. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:43: trailing whitespace.
+**Collinearity.** There is presence of collinearity if at least one column of the matrix $X$ is a linear combination of the other columns. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:48: trailing whitespace.
+**Regular set.** The regular set is the set of all columns that are not a linear combination of the other columns. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:95: trailing whitespace.
+#> 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:110: trailing whitespace.
+Now let us use the theorem to understand collinearity. Take one variable from the collinear set as the focal variable. By the definition of collinearity, the residual of this variable on all other variables is a $N$-vector of 0s. Indeed, since it is a linear combination of the other variables in the collinear set, it can be perfectly explained and there is no residual. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:114: trailing whitespace.
+But what does that mean for *variables in the regular set?* For any such variable, the residual on all other variables contains variation, and hence the coefficient is well defined. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:123: trailing whitespace.
+Now assume there is presence of collinearity and the model cannot be directly estimated. There are two cases. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:125: trailing whitespace.
+**1) The collinear set contains only control variables.** In this case collinearity is not an issue since it affects only variables that are not interpreted. The fix is easy: drop iteratively variables from the collinear set until there is no remaining collinearity (reconstruct the collinear set at each step). 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:127: trailing whitespace.
+Note, importantly, that it is not because it affects only control variables that collinearity is fine. You should always be wary: when there is collinearity in practice when it shouldn't in theory, this is often the sign of coding mistakes (either the raw data is wrong or the processing is flawed). Allow collinearity only when it makes sense in theory (we'll cover examples later [when collinearity is fine in theory](#subsec_removal_good)). 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:129: trailing whitespace.
+**2) The collinear set contains at least one focal variable.** *Now we have a problem.* Here collinearity can have two origins: i) the model is misspecified, or ii) the focal variables have been wrongly coded. If you are sure that the model is well specified, then check the data and fix how the variables are constructed. Now let's cover model misspecification. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:136: trailing whitespace.
+$$ 1 + 1 = 1 + 1 + (1 - 1) $$ 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:148: trailing whitespace.
+Let's take the following context: you study the publication process in science and want to quantify the gender gap in terms of publications. You are also interested in the effect of age on publications. You have a panel where scientists are indexed with $i$ and years are indexed with $t$. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:157: trailing whitespace.
+Is that all? Are we done with misspecification? Well, although the variable $age$ varies across time and scientists, it *is* in the collinear set. We can decompose this variable as follows: $age_{it} = year_{t} - birth\_year_{i}$. Now we can see that this variable is a linear combination of a scientist-invariant variable ($year_{t}$) and a time-invariant variable ($birth\_year_{i}$). This means that the variable $age_{it}$ can be fully explained by the scientist and time fixed-effects. Hence you cannot estimate the effect of age in the presence of these fixed-effects. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:159: trailing whitespace.
+**Any solution?** No. You need to change your model. In this case, the easy solution is to drop the scientist fixed-effects and accept the criticisms that may arise from not controlling for important time invariant confounders. To reformulate, if any of your focal variables is in a collinear set, you need to make non trivial changes to your model specification. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:172: trailing whitespace.
+**Sneaky misspecification.** One problem arises if the variable $harassed$ contains missing values and if, after removing observation with missing values, there is no more within-scientist variation in the variable $woman_{it}$. In that case, the variable $woman$ ends up in the collinear set when the variable $harassed$ is included in the model. This is not a misspecified model *per se*, but purely a data problem: the data does not support the model specification. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:176: trailing whitespace.
+The bottom line is that, even if the model is well specified, modifications of the sample, due to data availability for example, can lead *in fine* to model misspecification. As mentionned before, the problem is that econmetrics software can provide results even when the model is misspecified, so one really needs to be aware of the problem. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:183: trailing whitespace.
+Most econometrics software, be it in R, Stata, Python, etc, automatically remove collinear variables to perform the estimation. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:200: trailing whitespace.
+It is important to stress that we are not talking about *mathematical collinearity* but only about *numerical collinearity* which are two different beasts. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:203: trailing whitespace.
+Let us give two examples. In `R`, the function `lm` uses a QR decomposition of the matrix $X$ to solve the OLS problem. Linear dependencies (collinearity) are resolved during the QR decomposition. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:204: trailing whitespace.
+Differently from `lm`, the function `feols` from `fixest` resolves collinearity during a Cholesky decompostion of the matrix $X^\prime X$. While there are major advantages in terms of speed to use this method, it is known to be inferior to the QR method for collinearity detection because using $X^\prime X$ instead of $X$ reduces numeric precision. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:234: trailing whitespace.

docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:285: trailing whitespace.
+Ooopsie, our variable is now removed! This happens with the message: "The variable [...] removed because of collinearity". What happened? The algorithm could not invert $X^\prime X$ so removed the variable to be able to carry on. This is purely a numerical problem: remember that we store numbers with 64 bits, leading to at most 15 digits precision (so don't be suprised of the result of `1 + 1e16 == 1e16`). If the numbers were stored with 128bits precision, there would be no rounding error and no variable removal. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:291: trailing whitespace.
+#>   (Intercept)  I(x + 1e+06) 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:294: trailing whitespace.
+#> (Intercept) I(x + 1e+07) 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:310: trailing whitespace.
+Our coefficient of interest is $\gamma$ and we include scientist and institution fixed-effects.

docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:359: trailing whitespace.
+Coming back to our first question: Why is automatic variable removal a feature? In this example the scientist and affiliation fixed-effects *are just controls* and the researcher has no interest in interpreting them. Therefore collinearity is no issue here and it does not matter which variable is removed to fix collinearity. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:365: trailing whitespace.
+Automatic variable removal can be a problem **when your model is misspecified** and you haven't noticed it. 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:399: trailing whitespace.
+It just worked! We have coefficients for `is_woman` and `age`! Good news right, we thought our model was misspecified but in fact it's not: we can estimate the gender gap with fixed-effects! Or is it? 
docs/literature/raw/exec20_fixest_tech_8.txt:401: trailing whitespace.
+Well of course this is just a dream. If the model is misspecified in theory, it remains misspecified in practice: the estimates for `is_woman` and `age` do exist but cannot be intrepreted. To better see why let's replicate the previous estimation where we cosmetically modify the fixed-effects:
